\mathbb{E}[\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}]=\gamma_{j,k}\,\mathbb{E}[\psi_{j,k}^{h}]+\mathbb{E}[h_{j,k}^{-}]\,\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(\cdots)-\gamma_{j,k}]=\gamma_{j,k}\,\mathbb{E}[h_{j,k}^{-}]
(and similarly
\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\mathrm{full},k}^{\mathrm{prod}}]=\gamma_{\mathrm{full},k}\,\mathbb{E}[J_{\mathrm{full},k}]
), summing gives
\mathbb{E}[\psi(O;\eta_{0})]=\mathbb{E}[m(X;\eta_{0})]=\theta_{0}
.

Neyman orthogonality.

We must show
\partial_{\eta_{\alpha}}\mathbb{E}[\psi(O;\eta)]\big|_{\eta=\eta_{0}}=0
for each nuisance component
\eta_{\alpha}
. By the product score structure, it suffices to verify orthogonality of the component scores
\psi_{j,k}^{h}
and
\psi_{\mathrm{full},k}^{J}
with respect to
(P_{1,m},p_{m},p(z,X))
, and then show the product scores inherit orthogonality with respect to
\gamma
.

Orthogonality of
\psi_{j,k}^{h}
with respect to
P_{1,m}
.

Consider a perturbation
P_{1,m}\to P_{1,m}+t\,\delta P_{1,m}(X)
. Since
h_{j,k}^{-}
depends on
P_{1,m}
only through
J_{\mathrm{full},k}=P_{1,k+1}-P_{1,k}
, the pathwise derivative is

\partial_{t}\mathbb{E}[h_{j,k}^{-}]\big|_{t=0}=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\alpha_{P,m}^{(j,k)}(X)\,\delta P_{1,m}(X)\right],

where
\alpha_{P,k+1}^{(j,k)}=D_{j,k}^{-}
,
\alpha_{P,k}^{(j,k)}=-D_{j,k}^{-}
, and
\alpha_{P,m}^{(j,k)}=0
for
m\notin\{k,k{+}1\}
. Under
5.7
,
D_{j,k}^{-}=\mathds{1}(q_{j-1,k}<p_{k+1}-J_{\mathrm{full},k}<q_{j,k})
is well-defined. The IPW correction
D_{j,k}^{-}(R_{P,k+1}-R_{P,k})
in
\psi_{j,k}^{h}
contributes

\partial_{t}\mathbb{E}\!\left[D_{j,k}^{-}\!\left(\frac{\mathds{1}(Z\in S_{k+1})}{\pi_{k+1}}\bigl(YW-P_{1,k+1}\bigr)-\frac{\mathds{1}(Z\in S_{k})}{\pi_{k}}\bigl(YW-P_{1,k}\bigr)\right)\right]\bigg|_{t=0}=-\mathbb{E}\!\left[\alpha_{P,m}^{(j,k)}\,\delta P_{1,m}\right],

using
\mathbb{E}[\frac{\mathds{1}(Z\in S_{m})}{\pi_{m}}\mid X]=1
, so the two contributions cancel. The same argument gives
\partial_{P_{1,m}}\mathbb{E}[\psi_{\mathrm{full},k}^{J}]=0
(with
\alpha_{P,k+1}^{(\mathrm{full},k)}=1
and
\alpha_{P,k}^{(\mathrm{full},k)}=-1
).

Orthogonality of
\psi_{j,k}^{h}
with respect to
p_{m}
and
p(z,X)
.

The propensity scores enter
h_{j,k}^{-}
through three channels:

1.

the integration boundary
p_{k+1}-J_{\mathrm{full},k}
, corrected by
\alpha_{p,k+1}^{(j,k)}R_{p,k+1}

2.

the lower level-set boundary
q_{0,k}=p_{k}
when
j=1
, corrected by
\alpha_{p,k}^{(1,k)}R_{p,k}

3.

the alternative policy values
q_{j,k}
and
q_{j-1,k}
through the mapping
\phi(z,X,p(z,X))
, corrected by the policy terms involving
T_{j,k}
and
T_{j-1,k}
.

For channel (a), a perturbation
p_{k+1}\to p_{k+1}+t\,\delta p_{k+1}(X)
shifts the boundary by
\delta p_{k+1}
, giving
\partial_{t}h_{j,k}^{-}=-D_{j,k}^{-}\,\delta p_{k+1}
. The correction
\alpha_{p,k+1}^{(j,k)}R_{p,k+1}=-D_{j,k}^{-}\cdot\frac{\mathds{1}(Z\in S_{k+1})}{\pi_{k+1}}(W-p_{k+1})
contributes
\partial_{t}\mathbb{E}[\alpha_{p,k+1}^{(j,k)}R_{p,k+1}]=D_{j,k}^{-}\,\delta p_{k+1}
, canceling the plug-in derivative.

For channel (b), when
j=1
and
E_{1,k}^{-}=1
(i.e.,
p_{k}>p_{k+1}-J_{\mathrm{full},k}
), a perturbation of
p_{k}
shifts
q_{0,k}=p_{k}
, giving
\partial_{t}h_{1,k}^{-}=-E_{1,k}^{-}I_{1,k}^{-}\,\delta p_{k}
. The correction
\alpha_{p,k}^{(1,k)}R_{p,k}
contributes the matching cancellation.

For channel (c), a perturbation of
p(z,X)
for
z\in T_{j,k}
shifts
q_{j,k}
by
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial p}\,\delta p(z,X)
, contributing
I_{j,k}^{-}\,\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial p}\,\delta p(z,X)
to
\partial_{t}h_{j,k}^{-}
. The policy correction
I_{j,k}^{-}\sum_{z^{\prime}\in T_{j,k}}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial p}\frac{\mathds{1}(Z=z^{\prime})}{\pi(z^{\prime},X)}(W-p(z^{\prime},X))
cancels this via
\mathbb{E}[\frac{\mathds{1}(Z=z)}{\pi(z,X)}\mid X]=1
. Symmetrically, when
E_{j,k}^{-}=1
, the correction for
q_{j-1,k}
through
T_{j-1,k}
cancels the derivative
-E_{j,k}^{-}I_{j,k}^{-}\,\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial p}\,\delta p(z,X)
for
z\in T_{j-1,k}
.

Inheritance by product scores.

Since
\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}=\gamma_{j,k}\,\psi_{j,k}^{h}+h_{j,k}^{-}(\mathds{1}(\cdots)-\gamma_{j,k})
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(\cdots)-\gamma_{j,k}\mid X]=0
at
\eta_{0}
, any perturbation of
(P_{1,m},p_{m},p(z,X))
yields

\partial_{\eta}\mathbb{E}[\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}]=\gamma_{j,k}\,\underbrace{\partial_{\eta}\mathbb{E}[\psi_{j,k}^{h}]}_{=\,0}+\partial_{\eta}\mathbb{E}[h_{j,k}^{-}]\cdot\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(\cdots)-\gamma_{j,k}\mid X]}_{=\,0}=0.

The same holds for
\psi_{\mathrm{full},k}^{\mathrm{prod}}
and
\psi_{\Delta_{K}}
.

Perturbation of
\gamma_{j,k}
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{full},k}
.

The product score structure ensures orthogonality. By the Leibniz rule:

\partial_{\gamma_{j,k}}\mathbb{E}[\psi_{j,k}^{\mathrm{prod}}]=\gamma_{j,k}\,\underbrace{\partial_{\gamma_{j,k}}\mathbb{E}[\psi_{j,k}^{h}]}_{=\,0}+\mathbb{E}[h_{j,k}^{-}]\,\underbrace{\partial_{\gamma_{j,k}}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(\cdots)-\gamma_{j,k}]}_{=\,0}=0.

The same argument applies to
\gamma_{\mathrm{full},k}
and its corresponding product score.

Rearrangement.

Under
5.7
, the true values satisfy the monotonicity constraint
P_{1,0}(x)\leqslant\cdots\leqslant P_{1,K}(x)
for all
x
(since
J_{\mathrm{full},k}(x)\geqslant 0
). The rearrangement operator projects onto this monotonicity constraint pointwise in
x
. By the classical rearrangement inequality, for each
x
the rearranged vector
(\widehat{P}_{1,0}^{*}(x),\ldots,\widehat{P}_{1,K}^{*}(x))
is at least as close to the true monotone vector
(P_{1,0}(x),\ldots,P_{1,K}(x))
as the original
(\widehat{P}_{1,0}(x),\ldots,\widehat{P}_{1,K}(x))
:

\sum_{k=0}^{K}\bigl|\widehat{P}_{1,k}^{*}(x)-P_{1,k}(x)\bigr|^{2}\leqslant\sum_{k=0}^{K}\bigl|\widehat{P}_{1,k}(x)-P_{1,k}(x)\bigr|^{2}\quad\text{for all }x.

Integrating over
x
, rearrangement can only decrease the
L_{2}
estimation error:
\|\widehat{P}_{1,k}^{*}-P_{1,k}\|_{L_{2}}\leqslant\|\widehat{P}_{1,k}-P_{1,k}\|_{L_{2}}=o_{P}(n^{-1/4})
. The subsequent clipping
\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{full},k}^{*}\leftarrow\min(\widehat{J}_{\mathrm{full},k}^{*},\,\Delta p_{k})
is a projection onto
[0,\Delta p_{k}]
; since the true
J_{\mathrm{full},k}\in[0,\Delta p_{k}]
, this projection can only further reduce the estimation error by the same contraction argument. Therefore, all convergence rate assumptions on the nuisance estimators are preserved after rearrangement and clipping.

Second-order remainder bound.

Let
\Delta\eta=\widehat{\eta}-\eta_{0}
and write the linearization error as

R_{n}=\frac{1}{|I_{2}|}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\bigl[m(X_{i};\widehat{\eta})-m(X_{i};\eta_{0})-\nabla_{\eta}m(X_{i};\eta_{0})^{\top}\Delta\eta(X_{i})\bigr].

The functional
m(X;\eta)
is piecewise linear in
(J_{\mathrm{full},k},p_{k},q_{j,k})
through the
\max
and
\min
operators, and bilinear in
(\gamma_{j,k},h_{j,k}^{-})
. Under
5.7
, the
\max
and
\min
functions are locally linear at the true parameter values. The only second-order terms arise from the bilinear products
\gamma_{j,k}\,h_{j,k}^{-}
and
\gamma_{\mathrm{full},k}\,J_{\mathrm{full},k}
, yielding a remainder of order

R_{n}=O_{P}\!\left(\|\Delta\gamma\|_{L_{2}}\,\|\Delta h\|_{L_{2}}+\|\Delta\gamma_{\mathrm{full}}\|_{L_{2}}\,\|\Delta J_{\mathrm{full}}\|_{L_{2}}\right)=O_{P}(n^{-1/4}\cdot n^{-1/4})=o_{P}(n^{-1/2}).

Asymptotic normality.

By the standard DML argument
[
13
]
, the moment condition, Neyman orthogonality, and the
o_{P}(n^{-1/2})
remainder bound together imply:

\sqrt{n/2}\,(\widehat{\underline{\theta}}_{\omega,1}-\theta_{0})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n/2}}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\bigl(\psi(O_{i};\eta_{0})-\theta_{0}\bigr)+o_{P}(1)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,\,\sigma^{2}),

where
\sigma^{2}=\mathrm{Var}(\psi(O;\eta_{0}))
.

Consistency of the variance estimator.

Since
\widehat{\eta}\xrightarrow{P}\eta_{0}
and, under
5.7
, the score
\psi(O;\eta)
is continuous in
\eta
for
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{obs}}
-a.e.
O
(the set of discontinuity corresponds to
p_{k+1}(X)-J_{\mathrm{full},k}(X)=q_{j,k}(X)
, which has probability zero under the gap assumption), the continuous mapping theorem and the uniform law of large numbers give
\widehat{\sigma}^{2}\xrightarrow{P}\sigma^{2}
.
∎

F.2
Continuous Instrument

Proof of
Theorem

5.12
.

Recall the target functional from (
5.7
). Define
g_{1}(u,x)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{obs}}[YW\mid p(Z,X)=u,X=x]
and decompose the target as
\underline{\theta}_{\omega,1}=\mathbb{E}_{Z,X}[\psi_{1}(O)+\psi_{2}(O)+\psi_{3}(O)]
, where

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(O)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\int_{\min\{\underline{p}(X),q(Z,X)\}}^{\underline{p}(X)}Q_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{\underline{p}(X)}\right)\mathrm{d}v,

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(O)

\displaystyle\coloneqq g_{1}\big(\min\{\overline{p}(X),q(Z,X)\},X\big)-g_{1}\big(p(Z,X),X\big),

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(O)

\displaystyle\coloneqq y_{\min}\max\{0,q(Z,X)-\overline{p}(X)\}.

The plug-in estimator is
\widehat{\underline{\theta}}_{\omega,1}=\frac{1}{|I_{2}|}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}[\widehat{\psi}_{1}(O_{i})+\widehat{\psi}_{2}(O_{i})+\widehat{\psi}_{3}(O_{i})]
, where each
\widehat{\psi}_{j}
substitutes
(\widehat{p},\widehat{q},\widehat{\underline{p}},\widehat{\overline{p}},\widehat{g}_{1},\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X})
for the true quantities:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{1}(O)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\min\{\widehat{\underline{p}}(X),\widehat{q}(Z,X)\}}^{\widehat{\underline{p}}(X)}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{\widehat{\underline{p}}(X)}\right)\mathrm{d}v,

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{2}(O)

\displaystyle=\widehat{g}_{1}\big(\min\{\widehat{\overline{p}}(X),\widehat{q}(Z,X)\},X\big)-\widehat{g}_{1}\big(\widehat{p}(Z,X),X\big),

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{3}(O)

\displaystyle=y_{\min}\max\{0,\widehat{q}(Z,X)-\widehat{\overline{p}}(X)\}.

Main decomposition.

By the sample-splitting construction,
\widehat{\psi}
is fixed conditional on
I_{0}\cup I_{1}
and the observations
\{O_{i}\}_{i\in I_{2}}
are i.i.d. Decompose the estimation error as

\displaystyle\widehat{\underline{\theta}}_{\omega,1}-\underline{\theta}_{\omega,1}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{|I_{2}|}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\bigl(\widehat{\psi}(O_{i})-\psi(O_{i})\bigr)}_{\text{nuisance plug-in error}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{|I_{2}|}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\psi(O_{i})-\mathbb{E}[\psi(O)]}_{\text{sampling error}}.

Since
Y
is bounded (
2.2
) and
\omega
is bounded,
\psi(O)
has finite variance. By the central limit theorem, the sampling error is
O_{P}(n^{-1/2})
, which is dominated by the stated rate. By the law of large numbers conditional on
I_{0}\cup I_{1}
, the first term satisfies

\frac{1}{|I_{2}|}\sum_{i\in I_{2}}\bigl(\widehat{\psi}_{j}(O_{i})-\psi_{j}(O_{i})\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\widehat{\psi}_{j}(O)-\psi_{j}(O)\mid I_{0},I_{1}\bigr]+O_{P}(n^{-1/2}),

for each
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. It therefore suffices to bound
\mathbb{E}[|\widehat{\psi}_{j}(O)-\psi_{j}(O)|\mid I_{0},I_{1}]
for each component.

Preliminary: propensity score plug-in errors.

By
5.1
,
|\widehat{q}(z,x)-q(z,x)|=|\phi(z,x,\widehat{p})-\phi(z,x,p)|\leqslant C_{\phi}\|\widehat{p}-p\|_{\infty}=O_{P}(r_{p,n})
, where
C_{\phi}
is the uniform bound on
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial p}
. Similarly,
|\widehat{\overline{p}}(x)-\overline{p}(x)|\leqslant\|\widehat{p}-p\|_{\infty}
and
|\widehat{\underline{p}}(x)-\underline{p}(x)|\leqslant\|\widehat{p}-p\|_{\infty}
, so all estimated propensity-derived quantities are uniformly
O_{P}(r_{p,n})
-close to their population counterparts.

Analysis of the trivial bound
\widehat{\psi}_{3}
.

Since
|\max\{0,a\}-\max\{0,b\}|\leqslant|a-b|
, we have

|\widehat{\psi}_{3}(O)-\psi_{3}(O)|\leqslant|y_{\min}|\bigl(|\widehat{q}(Z,X)-q(Z,X)|+|\widehat{\overline{p}}(X)-\overline{p}(X)|\bigr)=O_{P}(r_{p,n}).

Analysis of the conditional expectation term
\widehat{\psi}_{2}
.

Define
u_{1}=\min\{\overline{p}(X),q(Z,X)\}
,
\widehat{u}_{1}=\min\{\widehat{\overline{p}}(X),\widehat{q}(Z,X)\}
,
u_{2}=p(Z,X)
, and
\widehat{u}_{2}=\widehat{p}(Z,X)
. The error decomposes as

\displaystyle|\widehat{\psi}_{2}(O)-\psi_{2}(O)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{l=1}^{2}|\widehat{g}_{1}(\widehat{u}_{l},X)-g_{1}(u_{l},X)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{l=1}^{2}\Big(\underbrace{|\widehat{g}_{1}(\widehat{u}_{l},X)-\widehat{g}_{1}(u_{l},X)|}_{\text{evaluation plug-in}}+\underbrace{|\widehat{g}_{1}(u_{l},X)-g_{1}(u_{l},X)|}_{\text{estimator accuracy}}\Big).

Stochastic Lipschitz property of
\widehat{g}_{1}
.

Since
\widehat{g}_{1}(u,x)
is piecewise linear in
u
on
\{u_{m}=m/M\}_{m=0}^{M}
, its Lipschitz constant is
L_{\widehat{g}}(x)=\max_{0\leqslant m<M}M|\widehat{f}_{u_{m+1}}(x)-\widehat{f}_{u_{m}}(x)|
. By the triangle inequality,

M|\widehat{f}_{u_{m+1}}(x)-\widehat{f}_{u_{m}}(x)|\leqslant\underbrace{M|g_{1}(u_{m+1},x)-g_{1}(u_{m},x)|}_{\leqslant\,\|\partial_{u}g_{1}\|_{\infty}\text{ by the mean value theorem}}+2M\cdot\max_{m^{\prime}}\|\widehat{f}_{u_{m^{\prime}}}-g_{1}(u_{m^{\prime}},\cdot)\|_{\infty},

where the first term is bounded by the regularity of
g_{1}
(
5.11
(2)). The grid-point error established below satisfies
\max_{m^{\prime}}\|\widehat{f}_{u_{m^{\prime}}}-g_{1}(u_{m^{\prime}},\cdot)\|_{\infty}=O_{P}(r_{p,n}/h_{n}+r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2})
, so

L_{\widehat{g}}(x)\leqslant\|\partial_{u}g_{1}\|_{\infty}+2M\cdot O_{P}\!\left(\frac{r_{p,n}}{h_{n}}+r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2}\right)=O_{P}(1),

where the last step uses
M(r_{p,n}/h_{n}+r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2})=o(1)
under
M\gtrsim\sqrt{nh_{n}}
and the nuisance rates being
o(M^{-1})
. Consequently,

|\widehat{g}_{1}(\widehat{u}_{l},X)-\widehat{g}_{1}(u_{l},X)|\leqslant L_{\widehat{g}}(X)\cdot|\widehat{u}_{l}-u_{l}|=O_{P}(r_{p,n}).

Convergence of the localized ML estimator.

We bound the pointwise error
|\widehat{g}_{1}(u,x)-g_{1}(u,x)|
at any fixed evaluation point
u\in[\underline{p}(x),\overline{p}(x)]
. Let
u_{m}
be the nearest grid point. Define

\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}=\arg\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\sum_{i\in I_{1}}K\!\left(\frac{u_{m}-p(Z_{i},X_{i})}{h_{n}}\right)(Y_{i}\mathds{1}(W_{i}\!=\!1)-f(X_{i}))^{2}

as the oracle ML estimator using the true propensity scores. By the triangle inequality:

\|\widehat{f}_{u_{m}}-g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)\|_{2}\leqslant\underbrace{\|\widehat{f}_{u_{m}}-\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}\|_{2}}_{\text{Generated Regressor Error}}+\underbrace{\|\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}-g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)\|_{2}}_{\text{Statistical ML Error $+$ Kernel Bias}}.

For the
statistical ML error and kernel bias
: let
R(f)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[K\bigl(\frac{u_{m}-p(Z,X)}{h_{n}}\bigr)(YW-f(X))^{2}\bigr]
denote the kernel-weighted population risk, and let
f^{*}(x)=\mathbb{E}[K(\cdot)YW\mid X\!=\!x]/\mathbb{E}[K(\cdot)\mid X\!=\!x]
be the unconstrained Bayes optimal predictor. Since the squared loss satisfies the Pythagorean identity
R(f)-R(f^{*})=\|f-f^{*}\|_{\bar{w}}^{2}
, where
\bar{w}(x)=\mathbb{E}[K(\cdot)\mid X\!=\!x]
defines a weighted
L_{2}
norm equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{2}
(the density of
p(Z,X)
is bounded above and below by
5.10
), the oracle inequality for empirical risk minimization
[
65
, Theorem 14.1]
applied to
\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}
over
\mathcal{F}_{n}
gives

\|\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}-f^{*}\|_{\bar{w}}^{2}\leqslant\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{n}}\|f-f^{*}\|_{\bar{w}}^{2}+O_{P}(r_{X,n}^{2}).

Since
g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)\in\mathcal{F}_{n}
by realizability (
5.11
), the infimum is bounded by
\|g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)-f^{*}\|_{\bar{w}}^{2}=O(h_{n}^{4})
, where the
O(h_{n}^{2})
bias of
f^{*}
relative to
g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)
follows from the twice continuous differentiability of
g_{1}
in
u
(
5.11
) via the bias expansion of Nadaraya–Watson-type kernel regression
[
61
, Theorem 1.1]
. The kernel weights localize the regression to an effective sample of size
\Theta(nh_{n})
(
5.10
), so the oracle inequality applies at this effective sample size. By the triangle inequality:

\|\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}-g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)\|_{2}\leqslant\|\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}-f^{*}\|_{\bar{w}}+\|f^{*}-g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)\|_{\bar{w}}=O_{P}(r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2}).

For the
generated regressor error
: the kernel weight perturbation satisfies

\left|K\!\left(\frac{u_{m}-\widehat{p}(Z_{i},X_{i})}{h_{n}}\right)-K\!\left(\frac{u_{m}-p(Z_{i},X_{i})}{h_{n}}\right)\right|\leqslant\frac{\|K^{\prime}\|_{\infty}}{h_{n}}\|\widehat{p}-p\|_{\infty}=O\!\left(\frac{r_{p,n}}{h_{n}}\right),

where
K^{\prime}
is bounded by
5.11
. Since the kernel-weighted regression is a ratio estimator with denominator bounded away from zero (
5.10
), this
O(r_{p,n}/h_{n})
perturbation of each weight propagates linearly to the weighted average, yielding
\|\widehat{f}_{u_{m}}-\widetilde{f}_{u_{m}}\|_{2}=O_{P}(r_{p,n}/h_{n})
.

Combining,
\|\widehat{f}_{u_{m}}-g_{1}(u_{m},\cdot)\|_{2}=O_{P}(r_{p,n}/h_{n}+r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2})
. The interpolation between grid points adds at most the grid resolution error, which is
O(1/M)=O((nh_{n})^{-1/2})
and is absorbed. Therefore,

|\widehat{g}_{1}(u_{l},X)-g_{1}(u_{l},X)|=O_{P}\!\left(\frac{r_{p,n}}{h_{n}}+r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2}\right).

Aggregating the evaluation plug-in and estimator accuracy contributions, and noting
r_{p,n}\leqslant r_{p,n}/h_{n}
for
h_{n}\leqslant 1
:

|\widehat{\psi}_{2}(O)-\psi_{2}(O)|=O_{P}\!\left(\frac{r_{p,n}}{h_{n}}+r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2}\right).

Analysis of the boundary quantile integral
\widehat{\psi}_{1}
.

Define shorthand
a\coloneqq\underline{p}(X)
,
\widehat{a}\coloneqq\widehat{\underline{p}}(X)
,
b\coloneqq\min\{\underline{p}(X),q(Z,X)\}
, and
\widehat{b}\coloneqq\min\{\widehat{\underline{p}}(X),\widehat{q}(Z,X)\}
. Adding and subtracting
\int_{b}^{a}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(v/a)\,\mathrm{d}v
, the error decomposes as

\displaystyle|\widehat{\psi}_{1}(O)-\psi_{1}(O)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\underbrace{\left|\int_{\widehat{b}}^{\widehat{a}}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{\widehat{a}}\right)\mathrm{d}v-\int_{b}^{a}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{a}\right)\mathrm{d}v\right|}_{\text{(I): limit and argument error}}+\underbrace{\int_{b}^{a}\left|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{a}\right)-Q_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{a}\right)\right|\mathrm{d}v}_{\text{(II): quantile function error}}.

Term (I): Limit and argument error.

If
\min\{a,\widehat{a}\}=0
, then
b\leqslant a
and
\widehat{b}\leqslant\widehat{a}
give
|\text{(I)}|\leqslant\max\{a,\widehat{a}\}\,\|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\|_{\infty}
. Since
\min\{a,\widehat{a}\}=0
implies
\max\{a,\widehat{a}\}=|\widehat{a}-a|=O_{P}(r_{p,n})
, we obtain
\text{(I)}=O_{P}(r_{p,n})
.

For
\min\{a,\widehat{a}\}>0
, the substitutions
t=v/\widehat{a}
and
t=v/a
yield

\int_{\widehat{b}}^{\widehat{a}}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{\widehat{a}}\right)\mathrm{d}v=\widehat{a}\int_{\widehat{b}/\widehat{a}}^{1}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\quad\int_{b}^{a}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\!\left(\frac{v}{a}\right)\mathrm{d}v=a\int_{b/a}^{1}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Define
G(c)\coloneqq\int_{c}^{1}\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t
, so that
\text{(I)}=|\widehat{a}\,G(\widehat{b}/\widehat{a})-a\,G(b/a)|
. Since
\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}
is uniformly bounded by
[y_{\min},y_{\max}]
(
2.2
),
G
is
\|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\|_{\infty}
-Lipschitz and bounded by
\|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\|_{\infty}
. We decompose

\widehat{a}\,G(\widehat{b}/\widehat{a})-a\,G(b/a)=\widehat{a}\bigl[G(\widehat{b}/\widehat{a})-G(b/a)\bigr]+(\widehat{a}-a)\,G(b/a),

so that
\text{(I)}\leqslant\widehat{a}\,\|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\|_{\infty}\cdot|\widehat{b}/\widehat{a}-b/a|+|\widehat{a}-a|\,\|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}\|_{\infty}
. The second term is
O_{P}(r_{p,n})
. For the first term, note that

\widehat{a}\left|\frac{\widehat{b}}{\widehat{a}}-\frac{b}{a}\right|=\left|\widehat{b}-\frac{\widehat{a}\,b}{a}\right|=\left|(\widehat{b}-b)-\frac{b}{a}(\widehat{a}-a)\right|\leqslant|\widehat{b}-b|+\frac{b}{a}\,|\widehat{a}-a|\leqslant|\widehat{b}-b|+|\widehat{a}-a|=O_{P}(r_{p,n}),

where the penultimate inequality uses
b\leqslant a
. Hence
\text{(I)}=O_{P}(r_{p,n})
.

Term (II): Quantile function error.

The substitution
t=v/a
yields

\text{(II)}=a\int_{b/a}^{1}\big|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)-Q_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t\leqslant\int_{0}^{1}\big|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)-Q_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t,

where the inequality uses
a\leqslant 1
and
[b/a,1]\subseteq[0,1]
. Note that this integrated form is precisely the conditional
W_{1}
distance between
\widehat{F}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}
and
F_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}
, so no uniform-in-
\tau
control is needed: integration absorbs any pointwise blow-up of the quantile error at
\tau\in\{0,1\}
against the vanishing Lebesgue measure near the endpoints. We decompose via the triangle inequality through the localized target
Q_{Y,\delta_{n}\mid X}
defined in
5.11
(6):

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\big|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)

\displaystyle-Q_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leqslant\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\big|\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)-Q_{Y,\delta_{n}\mid X}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\text{(II.a): statistical estimation error}}+\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}\big|Q_{Y,\delta_{n}\mid X}(t)-Q_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t}_{\text{(II.b): localization bias}},

where
Q_{Y,\delta_{n}\mid X}(\tau)
is the
\tau
-th conditional quantile of
Y
given
p(Z,X)\leqslant\underline{p}(X)+\delta_{n}
and
X
, as defined in
5.11
(6).

For (II.a), the estimator
\widehat{Q}_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}
is trained on the localized subset
\mathcal{I}_{\delta}=\{i\in I_{1}:\widehat{p}(Z_{i},X_{i})\leqslant\widehat{\underline{p}}(X_{i})+\delta_{n}\}
, where
\widehat{p}
and
\widehat{\underline{p}}
are estimated from the independent sample
I_{0}
. Conditional on
I_{0}
, the selection rule
\widehat{p}(Z_{i},X_{i})\leqslant\widehat{\underline{p}}(X_{i})+\delta_{n}
is a fixed (non-random) function of each observation, so the selected observations
\{(Y_{i},X_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\delta}}
are conditionally i.i.d. from the distribution of
(Y,X)
given
\widehat{p}(Z,X)\leqslant\widehat{\underline{p}}(X)+\delta_{n}
. Since
r_{p,n}=o(\delta_{n})
, the sup-norm error in
\widehat{p}
shifts the effective localization bandwidth by a negligible amount relative to
\delta_{n}
, so this conditional distribution is within
O(r_{p,n})
total variation distance of the target population with
p(Z,X)\leqslant\underline{p}(X)+\delta_{n}
. By
5.10
, the density of
p(Z,X)
near
\underline{p}(X)
is bounded away from zero, so
|\mathcal{I}_{\delta}|=\Theta_{P}(n\delta_{n})
. Applying the integrated quantile rate from
5.11
(5) to this conditionally i.i.d. sample of effective size
\Theta_{P}(n\delta_{n})
gives, uniformly in
X
,

\text{(II.a)}=O_{P}\bigl(r_{Q}(n\delta_{n})\bigr).

For (II.b), the
W_{1}
-Lipschitz smoothness condition in
5.11
(6) applied at
\delta=\delta_{n}
gives directly, uniformly in
x\in\mathcal{X}
,

\text{(II.b)}=\int_{0}^{1}\big|Q_{Y,\delta_{n}\mid X}(t)-Q_{Y,\underline{p}\mid X}(t)\big|\,\mathrm{d}t\leqslant C_{\mathrm{Lip}}\,\delta_{n}=O(\delta_{n}).

Combining Terms (I) and (II), and noting that
r_{p,n}=o(\delta_{n})
:

|\widehat{\psi}_{1}(O)-\psi_{1}(O)|=O_{P}\bigl(r_{Q}(n\delta_{n})+\delta_{n}\bigr).

Aggregation.

Combining the three components and noting that
r_{p,n}
is dominated by
r_{p,n}/h_{n}
(since
h_{n}\leq 1
) and the parametric rate
n^{-1/2}
is dominated by the nonparametric rates:

\displaystyle|\widehat{\underline{\theta}}_{\omega,1}-\underline{\theta}_{\omega,1}|

\displaystyle=O_{P}\!\left(\frac{r_{p,n}}{h_{n}}+r_{X,n}+h_{n}^{2}+r_{Q}(n\delta_{n})+\delta_{n}\right).

∎

Appendix G
Additional Simulation Details

G.1
Synthetic Experiment: Data-Generating Process

Continuous instrument.

The DGP has the following components:

•

Instrument:
Z\sim\text{Unif}(0,1)
, independent of everything else.

•

Latent resistance:
U\mid Z\sim\text{Unif}(0,1)
, independent of
Z
.

•

Propensity score:
p(Z)=\operatorname{logistic}(\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}Z)
with
\beta_{0}=-1
and
\beta_{1}=2
, so
p(Z)
ranges from
\operatorname{logistic}(-1)\approx 0.27
to
\operatorname{logistic}(1)\approx 0.73
.

•

Treatment:
W=\mathds{1}(U\leqslant p(Z))
.

•

Potential outcomes:
Y(1)=aU+\theta+\varepsilon
and
Y(0)=aU+\varepsilon
, where
a=0.5
,
\theta=0.5
, and
\varepsilon\sim\text{Unif}(-0.5,0.5)
. The outcome support is
[y_{\min},y_{\max}]=[-1,2]
.

•

Observed outcome:
Y=WY(1)+(1-W)Y(0)
.

The MTE is constant:
\text{MTE}(u)=\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid U=u]=\theta=0.5
for all
u
. The policy of interest is
q_{\alpha}(Z)=\operatorname{clip}(p(Z)+\alpha,0,1)
, and the target parameter is

\theta_{\alpha}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{q_{\alpha}}-Y]=\theta\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}(U\leqslant q_{\alpha}(Z))-\mathds{1}(U\leqslant p(Z))].

For small
|\alpha|
where boundary clipping is negligible, this simplifies to
\theta_{\alpha}\approx\theta\cdot\alpha=0.5\alpha
.

Discrete instrument.

The discrete setting uses a different DGP designed to highlight the performance of the closed-form bounds when the propensity score takes only finitely many values.

•

Instrument and latent resistance.

Z\sim\text{Bernoulli}(0.5)
(binary) and
U\sim\text{Unif}(0,1)
, independent of each other.

•

Propensity score and treatment.
The propensity score is piecewise constant with two levels:

p(Z)=\begin{cases}0.25&\text{if }Z=0,\\
0.75&\text{if }Z=1.\end{cases}

Treatment follows the threshold-crossing rule
W=\mathds{1}(U\leqslant p(Z))
.

•

Potential outcomes.
The control outcome is
Y(0)=0.1+0.22U
and the treatment effect is heterogeneous and increasing in
U
:
\tau(U)=0.48+0.18U
, giving
Y(1)=Y(0)+\tau(U)=0.58+0.40U
. Hence the MTE is
\text{MTE}(u)=0.48+0.18u
, which ranges from
0.48
to
0.66
. Measurement noise
\varepsilon\sim\text{Unif}(-0.1,0.1)
is added to the observed outcome
Y=WY(1)+(1-W)Y(0)+\varepsilon
. The outcome support used in estimation is
[y_{\min},y_{\max}]=[0,1]
.

The target parameter is
\theta_{\alpha}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{q_{\alpha}}-Y]
under the same uniform policy shift
q_{\alpha}(Z)=\operatorname{clip}(p(Z)+\alpha,0,1)
. For small positive
\alpha
, the new compliers at each instrument value have
U\in(p(Z),p(Z)+\alpha)
, so the ground truth is approximately

\theta_{\alpha}\;=\;\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0.25}^{0.25+\alpha}(0.48+0.18u)\,\mathrm{d}u\;+\;\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{0.75}^{0.75+\alpha}(0.48+0.18u)\,\mathrm{d}u\;=\;0.57\alpha.

Because the propensity score support consists of only two points
\{0.25,0.75\}
, the MTE is identified only on the interval
(0.25,0.75)
, and the IVOT and IVMTE bounds differ in how they treat the unidentified regions
(0,0.25)
and
(0.75,1)
.

Sample size and comparison.

The continuous instrument experiment uses
n=10{,}000
observations and the discrete instrument experiment uses
n=5{,}000
observations. IVMTE bounds are computed using the
ivmte
R package with moment constraints derived from the observed data. The PRTE weight function
\omega(u)
corresponding to the
q_{\alpha}
-policy is used for both methods.

G.2
Synthetic Experiment: Numerical Results

Tables

3
,
6
and
5
report the numerical bounds for selected values of
\alpha>0
, together with the IVMTE 95% backward confidence interval. For the discrete instrument setting, the IVOT 95% delta-method confidence interval is also reported. The IVOT bounds are uniformly tighter: in the continuous case by a factor of 5–8
\times
, and in the discrete case by approximately 2.4
\times
across all tested
\alpha
. For the discrete instrument, the IVOT 95% delta-method CI is also tighter than the IVMTE 95% backward CI. The IVMTE 95% CI is only slightly wider than the IVMTE identified set in most cases.

Finite-sample coverage: effect of sample size.

Tables

4
and
7
report the continuous instrument results at the smaller sample size of
n=5{,}000
(compare with
Tables

3
and
6
at
n=10{,}000
). While the IVOT bounds remain substantially tighter than IVMTE throughout, coverage drops below 100% for three values of
\alpha
near zero (
\alpha\in\{-0.02,-0.01,0.01\}
), yielding an overall coverage rate of
88\%
(22 out of 25 grid points). The near-misses occur where the IVOT interval is extremely tight (width
{\approx}\,0.002
) and the true
\theta_{\alpha}
falls just outside the estimated bounds due to finite-sample error in propensity score estimation. At
n=10{,}000
, coverage reaches 100% across all
\alpha
, confirming that this is a finite-sample phenomenon rather than a systematic bias.

G.3
Bed Net Application: Data and Setup

Dataset.

We use data from
20
, which studies insecticide-treated bed net (ITN) take-up among Kenyan households. The instrument is the offered price
Z
taking 17 distinct values spanning 0–250 Kenyan shillings. The treatment
W\in\{0,1\}
indicates ITN purchase and the outcome
Y\in\{0,1\}
indicates ITN usage at a one-year follow-up. The estimation sample consists of
n=1078
observations after merging the purchase and follow-up datasets.

Propensity score estimation.

The propensity score is estimated via logistic regression of
W
on
Z
, post-processed with isotonic regression to enforce the monotonicity assumption (higher price
\Rightarrow
lower purchase probability). The estimated propensity scores range from approximately
0.23
at the reference price of 150 KSh to
0.85
at zero price.

Policy and target.

The baseline policy corresponds to the reference price
z_{0}=150
KSh with
\widehat{p}(z_{0})\approx 0.23
. The alternative policy
q_{\alpha}
shifts the propensity score upward:
q_{\alpha}=\min(\widehat{p}(z_{0})+\alpha,1)
for
\alpha\in[0.05,0.62]
. The maximum
\alpha_{\max}\approx 0.621
equals the propensity at zero price minus the baseline propensity. The target is the policy-relevant treatment effect
\text{PRTE}_{\alpha}=\mathbb{E}[Y^{q_{\alpha}}-Y]/\alpha
, measuring the average per-unit effect of increasing compliance probability by
\alpha
.

G.4
Bed Net Application: Numerical Results

Table

6
reports the IVOT and IVMTE bounds for
\text{PRTE}_{\alpha}
at selected subsidy levels, together with 95% confidence intervals for both methods. IVOT yields tighter bounds throughout. Both methods indicate a positive and economically meaningful effect of price subsidies on bed net usage. The IVOT 95% delta-method CI rules out non-positive per-unit effects for all subsidy levels considered, whereas the IVMTE 95% backward CI remains inconclusive at small
\alpha
. At large
\alpha
(near the maximum feasible shift), the IVOT bounds nearly point-identify
\text{PRTE}_{\alpha}
, whereas the IVMTE identified set and its 95% CI remain substantially wider.

. The IVMTE point bounds reported in the table use degree-
10

u
-splines (degree-
20
results are discussed in the main text and shown in
Figure

). The IVOT 95% CI is computed via the delta method (influence function); the IVMTE 95% CI is a backward confidence interval from the

package based on the degree-

specification.
